Its demand pipeline reads type arguments off an explicit bracket, so a bare call arrives here with no instantiation to bind; the inference, the 123 measured files it is worth, and the shape of the missing piece are in bugs/FORMAL_a_bare_call_to_a_template_whose_type_arguments_are_inferrable.md. So write the operation in this module, or call a public function that does it — which is the same program with a definition this image can bind. Spelling it `FormatStruct[<a type>](…)` will carry the instantiation, and is a workaround for this gap rather than a correction to your code)
CODEGEN: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/algorithm/backend/tile.mojo  (build: tile: the body reads 'tile_size_list', its *-parameter, and this path has no variadic ABI. A formal value is one 64-bit word, so the arguments a caller passes past the fixed ones (the call may pass any number, against 3 fixed parameter(s)) have nowhere to be packed — a tuple of them is a container, and a container on this path is a frame-allocated blob in the frame of the function that built it, which the caller is not. Passing a variable number of arguments is a change to the calling convention both backends AND the Lean proof share. Take the arguments as named parameters, or read them from a list the caller passes)

CODEGEN: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/benchmark/compiler.mojo  (build: inlined_assembly['', NoneType, 'r,~{memory}', True] is a subscript whose index is a tuple. A value here is one 64-bit word and a list is a flat blob of words, so a tuple index has no representation on this path. It is one of two things: a lookup keyed by the tuple, which is lowered only when the base is a known dict and compares the key element-wise; or a two-dimensional index, which needs a row stride the source never states. Refused rather than computing a plausible flat index, and rather than using the tuple blob's own address as the index — that builds, runs, and returns an element nobody asked for.)

Spelling it `is_negative[<a type>](…)` will carry the instantiation, and is a workaround for this gap rather than a correction to your code)
CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/bit/mask.mojo  (build: mask.mojo imports 'std.sys.info', which cannot be built either: `FormatStruct` is called, and it is imported from `std.format._utils`, so the call has to bind a symbol `std.format._utils` exports. That module does not export it, and the reason is `doc/ABI.md`'s export rule rather than anything about this call: a name with a leading `_` is private, a generic template is not one symbol but one per instantiation (`_get_kgen_string[asm]()` is the measured case — both at once), an overload has no single symbol, and a C library name like `exit` or `write` is provided by libSystem. The exclusion is measured and settled in bugs/FORMAL_known_limits.md §1.1, and the generic case in §1.2. A generic template's instantiations ARE compiled into that module's library when an importer asks for them (`formal/monomorph.py`), so this call is one that asked for none — it names no type argument, or names one that is a value rather than a type, or spells the template as `module.FormatStruct`. **If the call names no type argument at all, the SOURCE is right and this path is short**: Mojo infers a template call's type arguments, so `FormatStruct(…)` with no bracket is correct code — the stdlib's own `FormatStruct(writer, "Allocation")` is spelled that way — and this path does not infer them yet. CODEGEN: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/builtin/len.mojo  (build: a `...` stands where this path needs instructions to emit, and there are none: a formal image is a compiled program, so every function it lowers must have a body. This is the language's own no-implementation marker — write the function, or declare it as a `trait` method, which is a declaration of an interface and so has no body to lower (a `...` in a trait method builds on both architectures, and a `...` in a plain function is refused whether or not anything ever calls it))
CODEGEN: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/builtin/none.mojo  (build: writer.write_string() is a method on a Writer — a multi-field struct, so on this path the receiver is the ADDRESS of a frame of 8-byte slots, not a file descriptor. It is not the `write` that is lowered: that one is honest only because open(...) makes its receiver a descriptor, and nothing here establishes that a Writer is one. Lowering it as a write would pass a frame address as fd(2): the syscall returns EBADF, nothing checks it, and the program's output is missing rather than wrong-looking. Refused rather than emitted as a call to a symbol spelled 'writer.write_string', which is what this used to do: the image built and then died in the loader)

Spelling it `FormatStruct[<a type>](…)` will carry the instantiation, and is a workaround for this gap rather than a correction to your code)

CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/builtin/rebind.mojo  (build: rebind.mojo imports 'std.memory', which cannot be built either: `FormatStruct` is called, and it is imported from `std.format._utils`, so the call has to bind a symbol `std.format._utils` exports. That module does not export it, and the reason is `doc/ABI.md`'s export rule rather than anything about this call: a name with a leading `_` is private, a generic template is not one symbol but one per instantiation (`_get_kgen_string[asm]()` is the measured case — both at once), an overload has no single symbol, and a C library name like `exit` or `write` is provided by libSystem. The exclusion is measured and settled in bugs/FORMAL_known_limits.md §1.1, and the generic case in §1.2. A generic template's instantiations ARE compiled into that module's library when an importer asks for them (`formal/monomorph.py`), so this call is one that asked for none — it names no type argument, or names one that is a value rather than a type, or spells the template as `module.FormatStruct`. **If the call names no type argument at all, the SOURCE is right and this path is short**: Mojo infers a template call's type arguments, so `FormatStruct(…)` with no bracket is correct code — the stdlib's own `FormatStruct(writer, "Allocation")` is spelled that way — and this path does not infer them yet. CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/collections/interval.mojo  (build: interval.mojo imports 'std.builtin.string_literal', which cannot be built either: `FormatStruct` is called, and it is imported from `std.format._utils`, so the call has to bind a symbol `std.format._utils` exports. That module does not export it, and the reason is `doc/ABI.md`'s export rule rather than anything about this call: a name with a leading `_` is private, a generic template is not one symbol but one per instantiation (`_get_kgen_string[asm]()` is the measured case — both at once), an overload has no single symbol, and a C library name like `exit` or `write` is provided by libSystem. The exclusion is measured and settled in bugs/FORMAL_known_limits.md §1.1, and the generic case in §1.2. A generic template's instantiations ARE compiled into that module's library when an importer asks for them (`formal/monomorph.py`), so this call is one that asked for none — it names no type argument, or names one that is a value rather than a type, or spells the template as `module.FormatStruct`. **If the call names no type argument at all, the SOURCE is right and this path is short**: Mojo infers a template call's type arguments, so `FormatStruct(…)` with no bracket is correct code — the stdlib's own `FormatStruct(writer, "Allocation")` is spelled that way — and this path does not infer them yet. Its demand pipeline reads type arguments off an explicit bracket, so a bare call arrives here with no instantiation to bind; the inference, the 123 measured files it is worth, and the shape of the missing piece are in bugs/FORMAL_a_bare_call_to_a_template_whose_type_arguments_are_inferrable.md. So write the operation in this module, or call a public function that does it — which is the same program with a definition this image can bind. Spelling it `FormatStruct[<a type>](…)` will carry the instantiation, and is a workaround for this gap rather than a correction to your code)

CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/collections/set.mojo  (build: set.mojo imports 'std.builtin.rebind', which cannot be built either: builtin_slice.mojo: is an `Optional[Int]`, and this target has no word to spell `None` as for it: every word is a value of `Int`. An `Optional` here is ONE 64-bit word holding the payload, so `None` has to be a word the payload cannot produce (`formal/model.py`'s `optional_none_word`, which is what answers `Optional[Bool]`, `Optional[String]`, `Optional[Int32]` and every reference-shaped payload from this same table). Answered anyway it would have to treat the integer 0 as the empty one, which is WRONG about `Some(0)` — measured on both architectures: `var z: Optional[Int] = 0; if z is None:` prints the empty branch. So this is refused rather than answered. What closes it is the OTHER representation: a tagged TWO-WORD value, `{tag, payload}`, where `Optional[T]` is the address of the pair — which is what `formal/model.py`'s `OPTIONAL_TWO_WORD` records, with the three measured reasons it is not a one-line change, and which covers every payload type including this one)

There is nothing an importer could bind, and nothing this backend could add.)

A parameter's value belongs to an INSTANTIATION: the use site supplies it as an argument (`TypeDict[…, <value>, …]`), and that argument is a value, not a literal written in this class body, so there is nothing here to write somewhere else. This path compiles one image for every instantiation — `doc/ABI.md` §Generics makes each instantiation a separate boundary symbol and there is no monomorphizer here — so at the class body there is no instantiation, and `Self.keys` names a parameter nothing has supplied. That is a different fact from a value this path cannot compute, and the two have different next steps: this one is closed by binding the parameter values at the instantiation site, not by editing the expression)

That module does not export it, and the reason is `doc/ABI.md`'s export rule rather than anything about this call: a name with a leading `_` is private, a generic template is not one symbol but one per instantiation (`_get_kgen_string[asm]()` is the measured case — both at once), an overload has no single symbol, and a C library name like `exit` or `write` is provided by libSystem. The exclusion is measured and settled in bugs/FORMAL_known_limits.md §1.1, and the generic case in §1.2. A generic template's instantiations ARE compiled into that module's library when an importer asks for them (`formal/monomorph.py`), so this call is one that asked for none — it names no type argument, or names one that is a value rather than a type, or spells the template as `module.FormatStruct`. **If the call names no type argument at all, the SOURCE is right and this path is short**: Mojo infers a template call's type arguments, so `FormatStruct(…)` with no bracket is correct code — the stdlib's own `FormatStruct(writer, "Allocation")` is spelled that way — and this path does not infer them yet. Its demand pipeline reads type arguments off an explicit bracket, so a bare call arrives here with no instantiation to bind; the inference, the 123 measured files it is worth, and the shape of the missing piece are in bugs/FORMAL_a_bare_call_to_a_template_whose_type_arguments_are_inferrable.md. So write the operation in this module, or call a public function that does it — which is the same program with a definition this image can bind. Spelling it `FormatStruct[<a type>](…)` will carry the instantiation, and is a workaround for this gap rather than a correction to your code)

That module does not export it, and the reason is `doc/ABI.md`'s export rule rather than anything about this call: a name with a leading `_` is private, a generic template is not one symbol but one per instantiation (`_get_kgen_string[asm]()` is the measured case — both at once), an overload has no single symbol, and a C library name like `exit` or `write` is provided by libSystem. The exclusion is measured and settled in bugs/FORMAL_known_limits.md §1.1, and the generic case in §1.2. A generic template's instantiations ARE compiled into that module's library when an importer asks for them (`formal/monomorph.py`), so this call is one that asked for none — it names no type argument, or names one that is a value rather than a type, or spells the template as `module.dealloc`. **If the call names no type argument at all, the SOURCE is right and this path is short**: Mojo infers a template call's type arguments, so `dealloc(…)` with no bracket is correct code — the stdlib's own `FormatStruct(writer, "Allocation")` is spelled that way — and this path does not infer them yet. Its demand pipeline reads type arguments off an explicit bracket, so a bare call arrives here with no instantiation to bind; the inference, the 123 measured files it is worth, and the shape of the missing piece are in bugs/FORMAL_a_bare_call_to_a_template_whose_type_arguments_are_inferrable.md. So write the operation in this module, or call a public function that does it — which is the same program with a definition this image can bind. Spelling it `dealloc[<a type>](…)` will carry the instantiation, and is a workaround for this gap rather than a correction to your code)

A `comptime` binding is compile-time state, which is why it cannot be a value that only exists while the function runs — an initializer over a parameter, or over anything computed, is not knowable here. Make the initializer a literal (or an expression of literals and other `comptime` names), or bind it with an ordinary `var` and read it at run time)

CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/memory/owned_pointer.mojo  (build: owned_pointer.mojo imports 'std.builtin.rebind', which cannot be built either: builtin_slice.mojo: is an `Optional[Int]`, and this target has no word to spell `None` as for it: every word is a value of `Int`. An `Optional` here is ONE 64-bit word holding the payload, so `None` has to be a word the payload cannot produce (`formal/model.py`'s `optional_none_word`, which is what answers `Optional[Bool]`, `Optional[String]`, `Optional[Int32]` and every reference-shaped payload from this same table). Answered anyway it would have to treat the integer 0 as the empty one, which is WRONG about `Some(0)` — measured on both architectures: `var z: Optional[Int] = 0; if z is None:` prints the empty branch. So this is refused rather than answered. What closes it is the OTHER representation: a tagged TWO-WORD value, `{tag, payload}`, where `Optional[T]` is the address of the pair — which is what `formal/model.py`'s `OPTIONAL_TWO_WORD` records, with the three measured reasons it is not a one-line change, and which covers every payload type including this one)

CODEGEN: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/origin/__init__.mojo  (build: the module-level comptime binding 'AnyOrigin' is initialized from an MLIR attribute template: __mlir_attr[`#lit.any.origin : !lit.origin<`, +mut._mlir_value, `>`] assembles an MLIR attribute from a template of backtick-quoted literal fragments and compile-time sub-expressions: it is not a subscript, and there is no MLIR on this path for the template to become. Refused rather than materialized as a container, which would turn an attribute into a pointer to a frame blob and disagree with the compiler that does have MLIR. Module-level `comptime` bindings are not part of the function-body expression walk on this path, so nothing used to refuse this and the binding read as an undefined name at every use site.)
CODEGEN: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/memory/pointer.mojo  (build: `strided_load` is called, and it is imported from `std.sys.intrinsics`, so the call has to bind a symbol `std.sys.intrinsics` exports. That module does not export it, and the reason is `doc/ABI.md`'s export rule rather than anything about this call: a name with a leading `_` is private, a generic template is not one symbol but one per instantiation (`_get_kgen_string[asm]()` is the measured case — both at once), an overload has no single symbol, and a C library name like `exit` or `write` is provided by libSystem. The exclusion is measured and settled in bugs/FORMAL_known_limits.md §1.1, and the generic case in §1.2. A generic template's instantiations ARE compiled into that module's library when an importer asks for them (`formal/monomorph.py`), so this call is one that asked for none — it names no type argument, or names one that is a value rather than a type, or spells the template as `module.strided_load`. **If the call names no type argument at all, the SOURCE is right and this path is short**: Mojo infers a template call's type arguments, so `strided_load(…)` with no bracket is correct code — the stdlib's own `FormatStruct(writer, "Allocation")` is spelled that way — and this path does not infer them yet. Its demand pipeline reads type arguments off an explicit bracket, so a bare call arrives here with no instantiation to bind; the inference, the 123 measured files it is worth, and the shape of the missing piece are in bugs/FORMAL_a_bare_call_to_a_template_whose_type_arguments_are_inferrable.md. So write the operation in this module, or call a public function that does it — which is the same program with a definition this image can bind. Spelling it `strided_load[<a type>](…)` will carry the instantiation, and is a workaround for this gap rather than a correction to your code)

There is nothing an importer could bind, and nothing this backend could add.)

A generic template's instantiations ARE compiled into that module's library when an importer asks for them (`formal/monomorph.py`), so this call is one that asked for none — it names no type argument, or names one that is a value rather than a type, or spells the template as `module.dealloc`. **If the call names no type argument at all, the SOURCE is right and this path is short**: Mojo infers a template call's type arguments, so `dealloc(…)` with no bracket is correct code — the stdlib's own `FormatStruct(writer, "Allocation")` is spelled that way — and this path does not infer them yet. Its demand pipeline reads type arguments off an explicit bracket, so a bare call arrives here with no instantiation to bind; the inference, the 123 measured files it is worth, and the shape of the missing piece are in bugs/FORMAL_a_bare_call_to_a_template_whose_type_arguments_are_inferrable.md. So write the operation in this module, or call a public function that does it — which is the same program with a definition this image can bind. Spelling it `dealloc[<a type>](…)` will carry the instantiation, and is a workaround for this gap rather than a correction to your code)

There is nothing an importer could bind, and nothing this backend could add.)

This path instantiates them on demand (`formal/monomorph.py`), so reaching this message means nothing asked this module for an instantiation of any of them: call one with its type arguments spelled (`S_ISBLK[Int](…)`) and the library you import will carry the instantiation this message says is missing. A BARE call still names no instantiation, and exporting the template under its base name instead would be wrong rather than conservative: one trie entry cannot be two instantiations, so a call with different type arguments would silently bind the first one's body.)

Its demand pipeline reads type arguments off an explicit bracket, so a bare call arrives here with no instantiation to bind; the inference, the 123 measured files it is worth, and the shape of the missing piece are in bugs/FORMAL_a_bare_call_to_a_template_whose_type_arguments_are_inferrable.md. So write the operation in this module, or call a public function that does it — which is the same program with a definition this image can bind. Spelling it `dealloc[<a type>](…)` will carry the instantiation, and is a workaround for this gap rather than a correction to your code)
CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/sys/__init__.mojo  (build: __init__.mojo imports '._io', which cannot be built either: _io.mojo: formal dylib has no public functions: _io.mojo exports nothing under doc/ABI.md's rules because it declares no function and no type at all — only module-level constants, which are inlined at their use site and cross no boundary. There is nothing an importer could bind, and nothing this backend could add.)

CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/testing/assert_aborts.mojo  (build: assert_aborts.mojo imports 'std.ffi', which cannot be built either: `dealloc` is called, and it is imported from `std.memory.alloc`, so the call has to bind a symbol `std.memory.alloc` exports. That module does not export it, and the reason is `doc/ABI.md`'s export rule rather than anything about this call: a name with a leading `_` is private, a generic template is not one symbol but one per instantiation (`_get_kgen_string[asm]()` is the measured case — both at once), an overload has no single symbol, and a C library name like `exit` or `write` is provided by libSystem. The exclusion is measured and settled in bugs/FORMAL_known_limits.md §1.1, and the generic case in §1.2. A generic template's instantiations ARE compiled into that module's library when an importer asks for them (`formal/monomorph.py`), so this call is one that asked for none — it names no type argument, or names one that is a value rather than a type, or spells the template as `module.dealloc`. **If the call names no type argument at all, the SOURCE is right and this path is short**: Mojo infers a template call's type arguments, so `dealloc(…)` with no bracket is correct code — the stdlib's own `FormatStruct(writer, "Allocation")` is spelled that way — and this path does not infer them yet. Its demand pipeline reads type arguments off an explicit bracket, so a bare call arrives here with no instantiation to bind; the inference, the 123 measured files it is worth, and the shape of the missing piece are in bugs/FORMAL_a_bare_call_to_a_template_whose_type_arguments_are_inferrable.md. So write the operation in this module, or call a public function that does it — which is the same program with a definition this image can bind. Spelling it `dealloc[<a type>](…)` will carry the instantiation, and is a workaround for this gap rather than a correction to your code)

CODEGEN: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/utils/_serialize.mojo  (build: p.unsafe_load() is Mojo's name for the UNCHECKED READ through a pointer — on a pointer the same operation `value()` is, at the same declared width, which is why it is in this table and reaches the same `dereference_lowering`. An ARGUMENT makes it a different construct: it reads at an offset from the receiver, and the address and the load are one decision there of which only the load half is answered, so `dereference_operands_refusal` names that rather than reporting an argument count. The receiver is not established to be a pointer here, and the answer a pointer would give is a load whose width the pointee decides: q = p.unsafe_value() makes q the value at p, which here is a Scalar[dtype] — a scalar, not a pointer, so q.value() is not a dereference. Refused rather than emitted at the only width this path could choose without one, which is 8 bytes: that over-reads a 1-byte pointee by seven bytes, returns a plausible number assembled from whatever follows it, and faults outright at a page edge. Declaring the pointee — `var p: Pointer[UInt8]`, a parameter annotated `Pointer[Int32]`, or a pointer-valued `external_call["sym", Pointer[T]]` — is what makes it answerable)

CODEGEN: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/utils/variant.mojo  (build: `FormatStruct` is called, and it is imported from `std.format._utils`, so the call has to bind a symbol `std.format._utils` exports. That module does not export it, and the reason is `doc/ABI.md`'s export rule rather than anything about this call: a name with a leading `_` is private, a generic template is not one symbol but one per instantiation (`_get_kgen_string[asm]()` is the measured case — both at once), an overload has no single symbol, and a C library name like `exit` or `write` is provided by libSystem. The exclusion is measured and settled in bugs/FORMAL_known_limits.md §1.1, and the generic case in §1.2. A generic template's instantiations ARE compiled into that module's library when an importer asks for them (`formal/monomorph.py`), so this call is one that asked for none — it names no type argument, or names one that is a value rather than a type, or spells the template as `module.FormatStruct`. **If the call names no type argument at all, the SOURCE is right and this path is short**: Mojo infers a template call's type arguments, so `FormatStruct(…)` with no bracket is correct code — the stdlib's own `FormatStruct(writer, "Allocation")` is spelled that way — and this path does not infer them yet. Its demand pipeline reads type arguments off an explicit bracket, so a bare call arrives here with no instantiation to bind; the inference, the 123 measured files it is worth, and the shape of the missing piece are in bugs/FORMAL_a_bare_call_to_a_template_whose_type_arguments_are_inferrable.md. So write the operation in this module, or call a public function that does it — which is the same program with a definition this image can bind. Spelling it `FormatStruct[<a type>](…)` will carry the instantiation, and is a workaround for this gap rather than a correction to your code)
CODEGEN: bootstrap_test_classes.mojo  (build: create_point returns a frame address, and the returned-frame convention needs a caller to reserve a block and pass its address — and create_point is this image's ENTRY, so its caller is the C runtime, which passes no such word. There is nowhere for the copy to land, and the address it would return names a frame that dies with this function. Return a field, or a copy of one, which is the same program with a lifetime this analysis can follow)
CODEGEN: bootstrap-validate.mojo  (build: main: `sources`, bound at line 84 to a value a function in this image handed back, is read again on line 89, after the call on line 0 has run. A container built in a function's frame does not outlive that function: this path has no heap for a list, so the blob is a block of that frame's own scratch ([count][element]… laid out per append SITE, `_blob_est` in each emitter), and the scratch is reclaimed the moment the function returns. The values read back here are whatever this function's NEXT call left in the same stack region — measured on both architectures, and the two give two DIFFERENT wrong answers for one source (arm64 reads the later call's scratch whole, x86-64 reads a mix of the two frames), which is the shape of the defect this backend exists to prevent rather than a value anybody can check. `bugs/FORMAL_listdir_no_run_time_sequence.md` item 3 records the capability that closes it (a container with a run-time length, `malloc`'d rather than frame-resident) and `bugs/FORMAL_a_returned_container_read_after_a_call_is_a_frame_reuse.md` records the measurement and the fix's shape. What lowers today: read the value before calling anything else, keep the container in the caller and pass a slot to fill, or take the elements as scalars one at a time)

A string here is a bare `char *` interned into read+execute __TEXT, so `"n=" + decimal(n)` has nowhere to be laid down at compile time (the field may be a runtime value) or at run time (there is no heap); this is the same missing buffer that keeps string concatenation and the length-dependent methods refused (see `string_concat_refusal` and LENGTH_DEPENDENT_METHODS). Print the parts as separate operands, or build the text with `+` once that is lowered.)
NOT-ANSWERABLE/HOST-IMPORT: formal/x86_64.py  (build: x86_64.py imports 'formal.model', which cannot be built either: model.py imports 'collections', which is a host module (CPython standard library), which has no Mojo source for this backend to compile; the record the callers here want is a STRUCT: declare it with its fields, and read them as fields. Its only module-level name is `namedtuple`, which builds a TYPE at run time, and there is no word on this path that denotes a type (doc/ABI.md, Generics), so `namedtuple("Census", "ok detail …")` asks for something that cannot be lowered even in principle — which is why "write a struct instead" is a complete answer for a record whose fields the compiler can see and is NOT an answer for one it cannot. Measured: five files in this repository's own tooling import it (`formal/lean.py`'s `Census`, `formal/model.py`'s `InitShape`, `tools/formal_sweep.py`'s three). `Counter` is a dict rather than a record, so it is the other question: see bugs/FORMAL_listdir_no_run_time_sequence.md)

The parser keeps the whole source token (`f"int64_t<opaque->{self.base}*>"`) as the literal's value, so what this build would have printed is the spelling: with `n = 7`, `print(f"n={n}")` printed `f"n={n}"` and exited 0 where CPython prints `n=7`. A string here is a bare `char *` interned into read+execute __TEXT, so `"n=" + decimal(n)` has nowhere to be laid down at compile time (the field may be a runtime value) or at run time (there is no heap); this is the same missing buffer that keeps string concatenation and the length-dependent methods refused (see `string_concat_refusal` and LENGTH_DEPENDENT_METHODS). Print the parts as separate operands, or build the text with `+` once that is lowered.)
CODEGEN: runtime/stdlib_wrapper.mojo  (build: main: `now` is called, and it is imported from `time`, so the call has to bind a symbol `time` exports. That module does not export it, and the reason is `doc/ABI.md`'s export rule rather than anything about this call: a name with a leading `_` is private, a generic template is not one symbol but one per instantiation (`_get_kgen_string[asm]()` is the measured case — both at once), an overload has no single symbol, and a C library name like `exit` or `write` is provided by libSystem. The exclusion is measured and settled in bugs/FORMAL_known_limits.md §1.1, and the generic case in §1.2. A generic template's instantiations ARE compiled into that module's library when an importer asks for them (`formal/monomorph.py`), so this call is one that asked for none — it names no type argument, or names one that is a value rather than a type, or spells the template as `module.now`. **If the call names no type argument at all, the SOURCE is right and this path is short**: Mojo infers a template call's type arguments, so `now(…)` with no bracket is correct code — the stdlib's own `FormatStruct(writer, "Allocation")` is spelled that way — and this path does not infer them yet. Its demand pipeline reads type arguments off an explicit bracket, so a bare call arrives here with no instantiation to bind; the inference, the 123 measured files it is worth, and the shape of the missing piece are in bugs/FORMAL_a_bare_call_to_a_template_whose_type_arguments_are_inferrable.md. So write the operation in this module, or call a public function that does it — which is the same program with a definition this image can bind. Spelling it `now[<a type>](…)` will carry the instantiation, and is a workaround for this gap rather than a correction to your code)

CODEGEN: t1.mojo  (build: sys.exit(): `sys` is a linked module but it exports no `exit`, so the call has no symbol to bind. What it does export: api_version, byteorder, flush_output, get_coroutine_origin_tracking_depth, get_int_max_str_digits, getdefaultencoding, getfilesystemencodeerrors, getfilesystemencoding, …. A name `doc/ABI.md`'s export rule excludes (a leading `_`, a generic template, an overload, or a C library symbol such as `exit` or `write`) is not advertised, so a module that defines one cannot be called by that name. The exclusion is measured and settled in bugs/FORMAL_known_limits.md 1.1; a module that needs to publish mutable state rather than functions is a different gap, written down in bugs/FORMAL_module_state_no_storage.md)
CODEGEN: t_argv.mojo  (build: main: sys.argv reads 'argv' out of the imported module `sys`, and a module is not a value this path can place: there is no register, frame slot or `__DATA` word for it because it is not one — it is the library on this link line, and what a dylib publishes is its FUNCTIONS (as symbols, so `sys.fn(...)` lowers) and its module-level names the build FOLDED TO A LITERAL (as values, so `sys.K = 1` lowers — there is exactly one value of a folded module-level name in a whole program, so the importer materializes the same one). `sys` publishes no 'argv', so there is nothing on the other side of the boundary to read — which is a DIFFERENT fact from "it is there and cannot cross", and the two have different repairs: a name the module's own functions write through `global` gets the sentence above, which names an accessor that works today, and a name it declares as a container gets the one above that, which names an element accessor. What `sys` publishes: api_version, byteorder, flush_output, get_coroutine_origin_tracking_depth, get_int_max_str_digits, getdefaultencoding, getfilesystemencodeerrors, getfilesystemencoding, …. `formal/hostmods/sys.mojo`'s own module docstring says which of those names it deliberately does not declare and why (`argv`, `stderr`, `stdout`, `modules`, `path`), and `bugs/FORMAL_module_state_no_storage.md` §(2) and §(4) record what would have to be true to close the boundary for the ones CPython has and this path does not)

Measured: five files in this repository's own tooling import it (`formal/lean.py`'s `Census`, `formal/model.py`'s `InitShape`, `tools/formal_sweep.py`'s three). `Counter` is a dict rather than a record, so it is the other question: see bugs/FORMAL_listdir_no_run_time_sequence.md)

CODEGEN: test_relaxed_imports.mojo  (build: '+' on two strings is refused on this path. A string here is a bare `char *`, so `+` is integer arithmetic on two addresses, and what it produced was measured: `print("ab" + "cd")` printed `[]` on arm64 and segfaulted on x86-64. Concatenation needs a buffer whose size is a function of both operands, and this path has no heap to allocate one in and no writable copy of the receiver's bytes to lay it down in — a string literal is interned into __TEXT,__text, which is mapped read+execute. This is the same missing buffer that keeps `upper`, `replace` and `join` refused; see LENGTH_DEPENDENT_METHODS)

NOT-ANSWERABLE/TARGET-LIMIT: test_sqlite3.mojo  (build: mojo_sqlite3_open is an entry point of the gimple backend's C runtime (the `mojo_*` ABI declared in runtime/*.h), and a formal image is freestanding: it links libSystem, whatever modules it imports, and nothing of runtime/ unless it names an entry point here — in which case `formal/build.py` puts the per-architecture `mojo_*` library on the link line for it. Its values are one 64-bit word. It could not be answered by linking that library either: its return value is `void *`, and that is an address rather than a value. An ARGUMENT can be one: this path carries the word and hands it straight back to the library that made it, never looking at what is behind it. A return is the other way round — the caller is the one who would have to read it, and the declaration does not say what is there. `void *` is spelled the same for a handle a program only passes on and for a container it reads elements out of, and a `MojoStr *` is a struct in the C heap, which a freestanding image has no allocator for. Either way the answer is a box, and it is not a box this path can produce. Refused rather than emitted as a call to a symbol nothing defines, which is what this used to do — the image built and then died in the loader with "Symbol not found".)

A string here is a bare `char *` interned into read+execute __TEXT, so `"n=" + decimal(n)` has nowhere to be laid down at compile time (the field may be a runtime value) or at run time (there is no heap); this is the same missing buffer that keeps string concatenation and the length-dependent methods refused (see `string_concat_refusal` and LENGTH_DEPENDENT_METHODS). Print the parts as separate operands, or build the text with `+` once that is lowered.)

NOT-ANSWERABLE/HOST-IMPORT: tools/control.py  (build: control.py imports 'collections', which is a host module (CPython standard library), which has no Mojo source for this backend to compile; the record the callers here want is a STRUCT: declare it with its fields, and read them as fields. Its only module-level name is `namedtuple`, which builds a TYPE at run time, and there is no word on this path that denotes a type (doc/ABI.md, Generics), so `namedtuple("Census", "ok detail …")` asks for something that cannot be lowered even in principle — which is why "write a struct instead" is a complete answer for a record whose fields the compiler can see and is NOT an answer for one it cannot. Measured: five files in this repository's own tooling import it (`formal/lean.py`'s `Census`, `formal/model.py`'s `InitShape`, `tools/formal_sweep.py`'s three). `Counter` is a dict rather than a record, so it is the other question: see bugs/FORMAL_listdir_no_run_time_sequence.md)

CODEGEN: tools/detach.py  (build: main: sys.argv reads 'argv' out of the imported module `sys`, and a module is not a value this path can place: there is no register, frame slot or `__DATA` word for it because it is not one — it is the library on this link line, and what a dylib publishes is its FUNCTIONS (as symbols, so `sys.fn(...)` lowers) and its module-level names the build FOLDED TO A LITERAL (as values, so `sys.K = 1` lowers — there is exactly one value of a folded module-level name in a whole program, so the importer materializes the same one). `sys` publishes no 'argv', so there is nothing on the other side of the boundary to read — which is a DIFFERENT fact from "it is there and cannot cross", and the two have different repairs: a name the module's own functions write through `global` gets the sentence above, which names an accessor that works today, and a name it declares as a container gets the one above that, which names an element accessor. What `sys` publishes: api_version, byteorder, flush_output, get_coroutine_origin_tracking_depth, get_int_max_str_digits, getdefaultencoding, getfilesystemencodeerrors, getfilesystemencoding, …. `formal/hostmods/sys.mojo`'s own module docstring says which of those names it deliberately does not declare and why (`argv`, `stderr`, `stdout`, `modules`, `path`), and `bugs/FORMAL_module_state_no_storage.md` §(2) and §(4) record what would have to be true to close the boundary for the ones CPython has and this path does not)

CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: tools/memcap.py  (build: memcap.py imports 'procrun', which cannot be built either: procrun.py: line 92: `(OSError, subprocess.SubprocessError)` is a handler arm with a body this path cannot put in the image, so it is refused rather than dropped: `formal` has no exception unwinder, so no edge runs from a raise site into an arm — a `raise` flushes the enclosing `finally` clauses and exits the process — and every statement in this arm (`ReturnStmt`) would be absent from the program that runs. Its effects would be silently missing: the image would build, exit 0, and not be the program you wrote, which is the one failure a value this compiler cannot detect on its own. An arm whose body is `pass`, `raise`, `continue` or `break` still builds — nothing is lost by leaving it out. Otherwise: move the work into the `try` body or after the statement (a `finally` if it is cleanup for the success path), or end the arm with `raise` if the program is meant to fail there.)
